Session 1: Setup and a First Look at Your Data

Project folders, packages, R basics, the pipe, and importing and inspecting data

⬇ Student script ⬇ Solutions ⬇ Whole workshop folder (ZIP)

NoteIn this session you will learn to
  1. Open the workshop’s project folder and explain why here() makes file paths work on any computer
  2. Install packages (once) and load them (every session)
  3. Create objects, call functions, and chain steps together with the pipe %>%
  4. Import a data file with import() and here()
  5. Inspect a dataset and write down a list of the problems that need cleaning

Before you start: complete the Setup and open scripts/Session 1 - Setup and First Look_STUDENT.R.

Part 1: Project folders and here()

Why keep everything in one project folder?

  • Organization: data, scripts, and outputs live in one folder with a predictable structure (raw_data/, processed_data/, scripts/)
  • Reproducibility: the project works on any computer — yours, a collaborator’s, future you’s
  • Shareable: zip the folder and send it, or put it on GitHub

Open the project folder for this class

  1. Download the workshop folder and save it somewhere you can find it (not your Downloads folder)
  2. In Positron, choose File → Open Folder…, select the CWML-R-Hands-on-Workshop-Series folder, and click Open. Positron uses that folder as the working directory
  3. Check the Explorer on the left: it should list the workshop files (raw_data, scripts, …)
TipYour own projects later

Make a new folder for each project (with subfolders like raw_data/ and scripts/), and open it in Positron with File → Open Folder…. Add an empty file called .here at the top so here() always finds it. (RStudio users: File → New Project does the same thing and creates an .Rproj file.)

Relative vs absolute paths

  • Bad (absolute): "/Users/mwegener/Desktop/maybe_here/data.csv" — only works on one computer
  • Good (relative): here("raw_data", "data.csv") — works on any computer that has the project folder

here() finds the top of the project folder (it looks for a .here file, which the workshop folder has) and builds the path from there.

here::here()                                   # where is the top of my project?
here::here("raw_data", "linelist_raw.xlsx")    # full path to a file inside it

here::here() means “the here() function from the here package”. The :: lets you use a function before loading its package.

Important✏️ TRY IT (1 min): a path to the practice data

Build the path to the practice data, raw_data/measles_raw.csv.

✅ Check: the output ends in .../raw_data/measles_raw.csv

NoteShow solution 
here::here("raw_data", "measles_raw.csv")

Part 2: Installing and loading packages

A package is a collection of functions someone else wrote. There are two steps:

Step Function How often Analogy
Install install.packages() Once per computer Buying a book
Load library() Every time you open R Taking it off the shelf

Install everything for the whole workshop series (skip this if you did the Setup):

install.packages(c("tidyverse", "here", "rio", "janitor", "skimr", "gtsummary", "flextable", "scales"))

Load only what you need today:

library(tidyverse)   # data wrangling (dplyr) and plotting (ggplot2), plus the pipe
library(here)        # file paths that work on any computer
library(rio)         # import() and export() for almost any file type
library(skimr)       # skim() for a quick data summary
library(janitor)     # get_dupes() today; clean_names() in Session 2
WarningCommon mistakes
  • Package names need quotes in install.packages() but not in library().
  • Red text is not always an error! Messages like “Attaching core tidyverse packages” are normal. An error starts with Error.

Part 3: R basics

Objects and the assignment arrow <-

<- stores a value in an object so you can use it later. Read it as “gets”. The object appears in the Variables pane.

my_age <- 35
my_age
[1] 35
my_age + 5
[1] 40

Vectors

c() combines values into a vector. Every value in a vector has the same type.

ages <- c(2, 18, 56, 61)
hospitals <- c("Central Hospital", "Military Hospital", "Other")

mean(ages)
[1] 34.25
length(hospitals)
[1] 3

Functions and arguments

A function does something to its inputs (arguments): function_name(argument1, argument2, ...). Arguments can be named.

round(3.14159)
[1] 3
round(3.14159, digits = 2)
[1] 3.14
TipGetting help

Type ?round in the Console to open the help page for any function.

Data frames

A data frame is a table: each column is a variable (a vector), each row is an observation. The tidyverse version is called a tibble.

patients <- tibble(
  name = c("Ana", "Ben", "Cho"),
  age  = c(34, 51, 27)
)
patients
# A tibble: 3 × 2
  name    age
  <chr> <dbl>
1 Ana      34
2 Ben      51
3 Cho      27
patients$age        # $ pulls out one column
[1] 34 51 27
WarningCommon mistakes
  • R is case-sensitive: Age and age are different
  • Text needs quotes ("Central Hospital"); object names do not (my_age)
  • Every ( needs a ) — Positron highlights the matching pair
  • # starts a comment; R ignores the rest of the line
Important✏️ TRY IT (2 min): objects and functions

Store three temperatures, 38.5, 39.1, and 37.9, in an object called my_temps. Then find their mean().

✅ Check: the mean is 38.5

NoteShow solution 
my_temps <- c(38.5, 39.1, 37.9)
mean(my_temps)
[1] 38.5

Part 4: The pipe %>%

The pipe takes the result on its left and passes it into the function on its right. Read it as “and then”. Shortcut: Shift + Cmd + M (Mac) or Shift + Ctrl + M (Windows).

# Without the pipe: read from the inside out
round(mean(ages), 1)
[1] 34.2
# With the pipe: read from left to right — "take ages, and then mean, and then round"
ages %>% mean() %>% round(1)
[1] 34.2
Note

You may also see |> — the pipe built into R itself. For this workshop they do the same thing.

Important✏️ TRY IT (1 min): the pipe

Use the pipe to find the mean of my_temps and round it to 0 decimals.

✅ Check: the answer is 38

NoteShow solution 
my_temps %>% mean() %>% round(0)
[1] 38

Why not 39? R rounds a 5 to the nearest even number (38.5 → 38), a rule used to avoid bias when rounding lots of numbers.

Part 5: Importing data

import() + here()

rio::import() reads almost any file type (.csv, .xlsx, .txt, SPSS, Stata, SAS, …). It works out the type from the file extension, so you only need to learn one function. Save the result with <-, or it only prints to the Console and is gone.

linelist_raw <- import(here("raw_data", "linelist_raw.xlsx"))

The same function works for other file types:

case_info <- import(here("raw_data", "case_info.csv"))   # comma-separated
hosp_info <- import(here("raw_data", "hosp_info.txt"))   # tab-separated text
WarningCommon mistakes
  • File names must match exactly, including capitals and the extension.
  • “Error: No such file” means the folder or file name is misspelled, or the workshop folder isn’t open (check the Explorer on the left).
TipOther import options you may see
  • Base R: read.csv() — older, slower, and needs more settings
  • readr: read_csv() — the tidyverse version, fast and returns a tibble
  • haven: read_sav(), read_dta(), read_sas() — SPSS, Stata, SAS files
  • Excel files with several sheets: import(here("raw_data", "file.xlsx"), which = "Sheet2")
Important✏️ TRY IT (2 min): import the practice data

Import the practice data raw_data/measles_raw.csv and call it measles_raw.

✅ Check: in the Variables pane, measles_raw shows 1215 rows and 11 columns

NoteShow solution 
measles_raw <- import(here("raw_data", "measles_raw.csv"))

Part 6: Inspecting data

Always look before you clean. Inspecting tells you whether the data imported correctly and what needs fixing.

Size and structure

view(linelist_raw)       # opens the Data Explorer (or click the table icon next to it in the Variables pane)
dim(linelist_raw)        # rows, columns
[1] 6604   12
names(linelist_raw)      # column names
 [1] "Case ID"         "infection date"  "date.onset"      "hosp date"      
 [5] "date of outcome" "OUTCOME"         "Gender"          "hospital"       
 [9] "age"             "age_unit"        "wt_kg"           "ht_cm"
Note🤔 Think about it: what is wrong with these column names?

Spaces, dots, capitals, and a mix of styles (Case ID, date.onset, OUTCOME). They are hard to type and need special handling. We fix all of them in Session 2 with one function.

In glimpse(), the type is shown in angle brackets: <chr> text, <dbl> number, <date> date, <dttm> date-time.

Note🤔 Think about it: which column types look wrong?
  • date.onset is <chr> (text) but the other dates are <dttm> (date-time)
  • age is <chr> — it should be a number
  • There is an age_unit column — are all ages in years?

Categories: count()

count() shows every distinct value in a column and how often it appears. It’s the fastest way to find misspellings.

linelist_raw %>% count(hospital)
                               hospital    n
1                       Central Hopital   11
2                      Central Hospital  457
3                            HMO Health  288
4                      Military Hopital   32
5                     Military Hospital  800
6                      Mitylira Hopital    1

10                          Regional MC   48
11              Regional Medical Center 1756
12 St. Mark's Maternity Hospital (SMMH)  417

14                                 <NA> 1509

  Gender    n

1   Death 2898
2 Recover 2213

linelist_raw %>% count(age_unit)
  age_unit    n
1   months   37
2    years 6567

Zooming in: select(), filter(), arrange()

  • select() keeps columns
  • filter() keeps rows that meet a condition
  • arrange() sorts rows (desc() for largest first)

Column names with spaces need backticks: `Case ID`. (The backtick key is at the top left of most keyboards.) Another reason to clean the names!

# Which patients have ages in months?
linelist_raw %>%
  filter(age_unit == "months") %>%
  select(`Case ID`, age, age_unit)

Missing values and duplicates

# Missing values in each column
colSums(is.na(linelist_raw))
        Case ID  infection date      date.onset       hosp date date of outcome 
            130            2315             286               0            1061 
        OUTCOME          Gender        hospital             age        age_unit 
           1493             317            1509             100               0 
          wt_kg           ht_cm 
              0               0 
# How many rows have no Case ID?
linelist_raw %>% filter(is.na(`Case ID`)) %>% nrow()
[1] 130
# How many rows are exact copies of an earlier row?
sum(duplicated(linelist_raw))
[1] 25
# Look at the duplicated rows (shows every copy, plus a dupe_count column)
get_dupes(linelist_raw)
Important✏️ TRY IT (3 min): missing values and duplicates

In measles_raw:

  1. How many rows have a missing `Patient ID`?
  2. How many rows are exact duplicates?
  3. Which column has the most missing values? Is that a mistake, or does it make sense?

✅ Check: a) 37 b) 15

NoteShow solution 
measles_raw %>% filter(is.na(`Patient ID`)) %>% nrow()
[1] 37
sum(duplicated(measles_raw))
[1] 15
colSums(is.na(measles_raw))
      Patient ID      Report Date         District              SEX 
              37               29               34               40 
     Age (years)      Vaccinated?            Fever             Rash 
               0                0                0                0 
        Temp (C) Days in Hospital          Outcome 
               0              766               68
  1. Days in Hospital has the most (766). That makes sense: patients who were never hospitalized have no hospital days. Missing is not always an error!

Part 7: The problem list

Here is everything we found in linelist_raw. This list is the to-do list for Session 2.

# Problem How we found it
1 Column names have spaces, dots, and capitals names()
2 Date columns are different types (date.onset is text) glimpse()
3 age is stored as text, and 37 ages are in months not years glimpse(), count()
4 Hospital names are misspelled (“Hopital”) or inconsistent (“Regional MC”), and many are missing count()
5 Gender and outcome have missing values count(), skim()
6 Impossible weights (negative, 0) and heights (4 cm, 295 cm) skim(), arrange()
7 130 rows have no Case ID is.na()
8 25 rows are duplicates duplicated()
Important✏️ TRY IT (10 min): the measles problem list

Write a numbered problem list for measles_raw in your student script, using everything you found in the TRY ITs. Save it — you will fix these problems in Session 2!

✅ Check: you should find at least 8 problems

NoteShow solution
  1. Column names have spaces, capitals, and symbols (? and parentheses)
  2. Age (years) is stored as text because of “unknown” (27 rows) and “999” (4 rows)
  3. District is misspelled (“Nroth”, “Cetnral”), has lower-case versions, and some say “… District”
  4. SEX is written 6 different ways (Male / male / M, Female / female / F)
  5. Vaccinated? is written 8 different ways (Yes / yes / Y, No / no / N / NO, Unknown)
  6. Outcome has inconsistent capitalization and 68 missing values
  7. Some temperatures were entered in Fahrenheit (values around 97–105)
  8. 37 rows have no Patient ID
  9. 15 rows are duplicates
  10. Report Date, District, and SEX have some missing values
Tip⭐ Bonus: your own data

Brought your own dataset? Put the file in the raw_data/ folder, then: import() it; look at it with dim(), glimpse(), and skim(); count() its text columns to look for misspellings; check for missing values and duplicates; and write its problem list.

Key points

  • A project folder plus here() makes file paths work on any computer.
  • Install a package once with install.packages(); load it every session with library().
  • <- saves a value in an object; c() makes a vector; functions take arguments.
  • The pipe %>% means “and then”, so code reads from left to right.
  • import(here("folder", "file")) reads almost any file type.
  • Look before you clean: glimpse(), skim(), count(), filter()/select()/arrange(), is.na(), and duplicated() find the problems.

Next: Session 2 — Cleaning. We fix the problem list with clean_names(), mutate(), if_else(), and case_when().
